ParlaMint-DK_20160119130114	Thank you. Since this is the first Question Time in 2016, I would like to begin by wishing the members of the Danish Parliament a happy New Year and expressing the wish for good cooperation in the year we have started, which is of course marked by major tasks, and the largest of them is in the migration and refugee situation by its very nature. There is no doubt about that. I myself described it in my New Year's speech as perhaps the biggest political crisis of this century... big words that I only take in my mouth because I mean them. Our ability to deal with this crisis will be crucial to how our society develops in the years to come. We must be able to recognise Denmark both during the crisis and when it is over. This is, of course, about the number of refugees, which is why we have tightened the conditions, but it is also about solving the integration task better than we have done historically, for the sake of the individual refugee's ability to have a good life, for the sake of the cohesion of our society and for the sake of the economy. It is not a task that can be solved here at Christiansborg in isolation, but it calls for all the forces in our society, and it is also the reason why the government invites parties outside Christiansborg to negotiate with the municipalities and tripartite discussions with the trade unions and employers' organisations to promote, in short, better integration, where the refugees who are to be with us, at least for a time, will be able to become self-supporting. We must also focus on growth throughout Denmark in 2016. Before Christmas, the government concluded an agreement on better conditions for the entire food and agricultural industry. The next step will be to conclude the negotiations that are currently under way on a new and fairer planning law, and the other initiatives in the government's growth initiative that can promote development opportunities throughout Denmark. Focusing on better education is also an area that we need to address in 2016. We have, of course, made broad agreements under the previous government on the reorganization of the primary school, and we have made agreements on the reorganization of our vocational training. This year the government will come up with a proposal for a reform of our high schools, which will make our young people better equipped to start higher education. I think we must also recognise that it is not enough that we have now looked at youth education one by one, but there is a need to take a look at them all together. There are three major challenges. Firstly, many young people do not get the right youth education for the first time, but have to take two or more. Secondly, far too many people fall away; on vocational training it is about half. Thirdly, some go to production school or a similar offer, but many have a limited benefit. It wears out the motivation, and we can see that after 7 years after completing the 9th grade it is 20 per cent. of those young people who have not even started a youth education programme. As I said in my New Year's speech, we must take it particularly seriously; the young people who lose their grip on their schooling risk ultimately losing their grip on life. It doesn't have to be like that. It shouldn't be like that. Now, knowing that it is possible to turn defeat into success against all odds with the right effort, it is double sad that many young people do not get that opportunity. For that is a human cost to the individual, and it is also an economic cost to society, because without education you risk becoming a social client. Basically, this also threatens the cohesion of our country. That is why yesterday the Minister of Education and I had a first meeting with a group of insightful people to discuss how we better give young people a constructive boost, a meeting that gave good inspiration for the future work, which among other things. This means that later this week the government will set up a group of experts on better ways of giving more young people after primary school the upper secondary education that suits them. It is my expectation that during 2016 we will be able to return to discussions here in Parliament and with the Danish Parliament's political parties on new legislation, etc. that will support it, so that more young people in the future will not only receive youth education, but that they will receive the right youth education and the right youth education for the first time than is the case today. With that, I look forward not only to the question hour here today, but to a year of work here in the Danish Parliament.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119145340	Here we are discussing a bill on a green housing job scheme, and just to sketch out the historical course, it is the case that shortly after the election an agreement was made between the Left, the Danish People's Party, the SF, the Alternative and the Conservative People's Party, which means that one continued with the well-known model in 2015, whereas one then agreed that unlike it was supposed to be a more green model here in 2016 and 2017. It has to be said clearly that in the past the housing job scheme has been mostly meant as a business cycle tool, whereas now it has to support a more green development, where you want to motivate homeowners to invest in energy-efficient measures in their private homes. One thing that has also come along and that one cannot therefore qualify as green, but which I certainly think is very positive, is that people can now get deductions for the installation of fast broadband, which should help them get better coverage. It is a system that is technology-neutral and respects market forces and that helps to support citizens so that they have a greater chance of getting a fast broadband connection. I think there is a need to do something about this here in Christiansborg, given that there are some places where the market is failing, and in that case there should be a helping hand for those who are hit by it and who will then have a faster connection. One cannot say that it is a green approach, but there is, after all, a touch of it, in that it may be able to motivate people to a greater extent to work from home and not to start the car, or because they may be able to act without having to drive. This is not something that I would describe as a green approach, but, after all, as a positive approach. The new system also differs from the old one in a different way. In the past, it was possible to obtain a deduction of DKK 15,000, which could be used either on services or on the artisan part, whereas the new scheme is divided up in such a way that the services of the craftsman can be deducted from DKK 12,000. from, and on the services DKK 6,000. The last part of the services we in the Danish People's Party are happy to participate. This is among other things. the part that gives a helping hand to children's families with childcare or to people who have to do some gardening, or to the pensioner who needs some extra cleaning. It is also mainly there the effect in relation to undeclared work lies. So at least we think it's a good thing that you still have that part with you after all. Finally, I should also like to mention one thing which is actually part of the agreement but which is not part of the legislative proposal. This is the part of the agreement where we actually get a little funding to get more research done in the experience of the Swedish scheme. I am thinking in particular of the part of the Swedish scheme where you have a higher deduction on the service part, i.e. cleaning, gardening, etc. Here, Sweden has good experience of reducing the amount of undeclared work. There we look forward very much to a study now being carried out and that we get some more qualitative data that we here in the Danish Parliament can use in the further work to find out if you might have to make a new model when this model expires, in order to get a good tool to combat undeclared work. However, as I said in the first place, this is a proposal and an agreement that the Danish People's Party was in the circle around, so we can of course support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119151119	The proposal for the green housing job scheme is what we are discussing today, and in many ways it is a very positive proposal. In concrete terms, this is done by the fact that the rate of adjustment, which is determined each year and which this year should have been 6,6 ■ that is what the maximum amount of the debt must be increased by 0 this year. As I have said, this is a wish from the Conservatives to the Finance Act, but we really share many of the views behind it, namely that there have been a number of sharp increases in some areas which may be problematic for, among others, those areas. children's families who were also part of the debate just before and there are pensioners who bought their house in good faith perhaps 20 or 30 years ago and who have planned their finances after they can live in the house when they got older, who are now being squeezed out because of an increasing ground debt. So we can see the problem behind it, and we really think it makes sense to give the homeowners a breathing space of one year. It is a breathing space at a time when the property assessment system has been put out of effect, among other things. Because, as some may remember, there was a report from the Reichsaudit which showed that 75%. The property valuations were wrong. Some were too low, and some were too high. We therefore think that, when we are on the steps to create a new assessment system, it makes sense to give them a breathing space for one year after all. It is therefore necessary to include in the discussion that this is one year, because afterwards the same houseowners will have an increase in exactly the same rate of growth until they hit the ceiling. One must also take part in the discussion that when you look at the economic effects of this bill, they turn out to be limited in that the lasting impact of the bill is 75 million. kr. Of course, that may sound like a lot, but in relation to a property valuation tax system, which, as far as I remember, is worth 42 billion. It is perhaps not here that one should inflate oneself to say that it is a huge tax credit to the very richest when it comes to one year and it is a postponement of a tax increase of just one year. ParlaMint-DK_20160119175243	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for Social Affairs and the Interior.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119175310	Thank you for that, President. We are now discussing the first of four motions for resolutions tabled by the Danish People's Party. The first motion for a resolution, B 32, intends to require the government to submit before the end of the 2015-16 parliamentary year a draft law amending electoral law in two areas. First of all, the proposal is that people who need help to vote for an election should be able to choose for themselves what help they want. Today, it is the case that all voters who wish to be helped to vote need the help of two people. It must be either two officials, i.e.: electoral rulers or appointed voters or an official and a helper elected by the electorate. With this proposal, the electorate must be able to decide for himself whether or not to participate in the vote when the electorate has chosen to have the help of a personally appointed helper. The second part of the proposal is that special or electronic aids should be made available in the voting box when there is a choice. The point is that it must be easier for people with disabilities to be able to vote without the help of other people, i.e. people with disabilities. officials and personally appointed assistants. Let me start by saying that, from the government's point of view, this is a very good proposal. We will support the substance of the proposal, but I also have to say that it is not completely uncomplicated and that it will therefore not be possible to put forward the legislative proposal in this session in practice. It will be practically possible to put it forward in the next parliamentary year, but I will come back to that. I certainly hope that we will be able to find common ground here in the Folketing, both on the substance of the proposal, but indeed on the implementation of the proposal. Let me start with the first part of the proposal, which deals with helping the vote itself. Today, when a voter needs help to cast his vote, the aid must always be provided, as it is now, by two people. So it must be either two officials or an official and a personally appointed helper. There are arguments in this way that the rules are designed as they are, and it was a very broad majority behind the rules that we have today. The fact that an official is always present counteracts the risk that the electorate will be exposed to undue influence. This will also help to ensure public confidence in our electoral system. However, in the vast majority of cases where a voter has chosen a personal helper, this risk of undue influence will not be present at all. It must be assumed that, in the vast majority of cases, the electorate chooses a person whom he or she trusts to help him, for example. the spouse, a family member or a good friend. The government agrees with the proposer that it may seem intimidating to the individual choose that there must always be an authority person inside the voting box when the voter needs help. Experience with the rules has also shown that a number of voters do not want an official present. Against this background, the Government considers that there are at least equally serious considerations, if not more serious considerations in favour of amending these rules. Therefore, we are prepared to change the rules in such a way that if the electorate indicates directly and unequivocally to an electoral leader or an appointed electorate that the electorate only wants the help of his personally appointed helper, then there should not also be an official. If the electorate cannot make that known, the aid must be provided as it has been so far. This seems to us to be a fair balance of consideration for the freedom of the individual electorate to avoid undue influence on the electorate and also to ensure public confidence in our electoral system. When it comes to the part of the proposal which relates to the provision of special or electronic aids in the voting box, I can only say that this proposal is as though it were taken out of my own mouth, because the government is concerned about how to improve the opportunities for people with disabilities or disability in order to be able to vote on its own behalf. Before the government can put forward such a bill, it is of course important that we also have a dialogue with relevant disability organisations so that together we can find solutions that can actually help those who need it. And it is important that the solutions that we find are practical, but also that they take account of people's confidence in the electoral process itself. So we are looking at what opportunities there are and what experiences have been gained elsewhere. I can say that the officials of the Ministry have already begun this dialogue with the relevant disability organisations. In conclusion, I repeat that this is a good proposal, or two good proposals in one, and we support it. As regards the timing of the legislative proposal, I can reassure you that the next planned elections are, of course, the municipal and regional elections to be held in November 2017. So the changes in the law will be able to come into force well before this election, even though the submission will only take place until the next parliamentary year.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119182116	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for Social Affairs and the Interior. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119182142	The motion for a resolution we are debating now wants to oblige the government to make it a duty for the municipalities to use their websites to provide information on the time limits for processing in the social field. The Danish People's Party tabled a similar motion for a resolution in the last session, B 128, in which I myself was then the rapporteur of the Left in this area, and I would like to start by saying, as I did at the time, that I think it is a good and sensible objective behind the proposal. The government basically believes that the public is there for the citizens, not the other way around, and therefore it is also important that the municipalities are good at communicating with the citizens. When it comes to processing times in the social field, citizens should easily be able to obtain information on the level of service they can expect from their municipality. But it will certainly come as no surprise that, although I believe that the intention behind the motion for a resolution is correct, the position of the government on the proposal will be the same as that of the Left in the last session of B 128, namely that we cannot support the motion for a resolution, and there are two reasons for this. First of all, as you know, the municipalities are already obliged to set deadlines for the duration of the proceedings in the individual cases, and the time limits for publication are the obligation of the municipality. So the responsibilities of the municipalities in this area are quite clear. The second reason is that the government basically believes that as far as possible we should leave it to the municipalities themselves to assess which methods are best suited to achieving the objectives that we set out from the central point of view. In relation to the length of proceedings in the social field, I can point out the conditions set out in the preparatory work on the rules in force. It is provided that the time limits are published by the municipalities in a manner that is customary in each municipality and that interested citizens have good opportunities to familiarise themselves with the deadlines. Within this framework, the individual municipal council has the freedom to decide how to publish in the municipality concerned, and in more detail I do not believe in principle that we in the Folketing should legislate here. I have then noted that many municipalities actually publish the processing deadlines on their website. I think this is quite natural as well, but I am not so concerned that the 98 municipalities do not necessarily all publish exactly the same way. What is important is that the deadlines are to be found and, ultimately, that matters are actually dealt with on time. It means, as I see it, less whether the deadlines are published in one way or in another way in the neighbouring municipality. I understand that in many contexts it is nice to have this great overview, as from the central point of view, but on the other hand we should not be blind to the fact that citizens are different and that we also have different needs. As I said, I agree with the proposers that the websites of the municipalities appear to be an obvious place to publish the time limits for the proceedings. This is where a great deal of other information for citizens is already posted, but it is not all citizens who are equally good at using the digital information sources, and I can therefore imagine that there may be other ways of communicating that can be equally appropriate to some groups of citizens. If you compare municipalities in a sample which actually publish their processing deadlines on the website, there is also a difference in how you do it and how easy it is to find the information on the many pages you can click through. In fact, a rule such as that put forward by the authors of the motion for a resolution will not be able to change that. Such a rule will therefore not be a guarantee that all municipalities will follow the best examples. If it is to be made easier for citizens to find the time-limits for cases in municipalities that may not be so good at communicating them today, then there must be wider communication tools in play, and I think that this kind of challenge is best solved by dialogue and not by centrally established procedural rules. So I would like to take this opportunity to call on the municipalities to take a critical look at their communication efforts to the citizens in this area. Is that okay? Yeah, maybe it's not everywhere. At the same time, I would urge citizens and associations to seek to influence the municipalities if they have certain wishes for the way in which the municipality has to publish their deadlines. So the government shares the objective behind the resolution, but we believe that the proposed remedy is neither necessary nor appropriate, and therefore the government cannot support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119183042	For social democracy, it is important to ensure that we have active local democracy and municipal autonomy, and of course it is within a framework. But the interest in social relations is, of course, very much linked to the mutual and political activity created by experiences in the vicinity of citizens. The municipality is the public authority, which in many respects is closest to the citizens, and we would really like to create the best platforms for citizens to get involved in the place in which they live. I think the municipal authorities are well aware that they come the longest with citizens who experience themselves involved in local government through information on the possibilities of involvement. The Danish Parliament has already provided for citizens to be informed about processing times, because it is an important part to assess the efficiency and respect of the municipality for its citizens. We are therefore not prepared to make further demands which the municipalities must comply with. ParlaMint-DK_20160119183518	From the heart, thank you. Because we have legislation which requires the municipalities to publish the processing times. That is what the authors of the motion for a resolution themselves write in the justification for their proposals. The municipalities are obliged to set and publish the deadlines from the receipt of an application to a decision, and if the deadline is not met, applicants must be informed in writing of when to expect a decision. However, we do not have any legislation which will ensure the uniformity of exactly how publication is to take place, as the authors of the motion for a resolution want. It is up to the municipalities to decide for themselves, and the Left believes it should continue to be. We focus on results and not on process, and if the individual municipality has a special way of doing things that work, then we in the Left see no reason to have centrally established rules on the method in this area, but in 2016, when we have gradually got used to the fact that the Internet is probably something that has come to stay, I simply cannot imagine the municipality that should not choose to publish it on the website. That's the easiest, the simplest and the cheapest to do. Why should you sit down and make a brochure and distribute it to all the libraries or have a man stand down at City Hall Square with a bell and announce that the municipality's processing times are so and so long? It's on the websites, and if it's not on the website, it's my claim that it's because the municipality doesn't live up to the law. And then you have to grab them, and you can do that in many ways. The easiest would be just to write an e-mail to the committee chairman or the mayor saying, "Look, where are these processing times?" And, of course, it is hoped that the politician in question will have red ears. So in the Left, we simply do not think that we need to set in motion a huge legislative process in order to ensure something that should already happen. Well, if you don't publish it on the website, where the hell would you do it? So we agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution on the objective, but not on the means, which is, of course, that we should embark on a legislative process for a tiny thing, which is going on in advance, and that is why the Left cannot support the motion for a resolution.

It is important for citizens to be able to keep up with these times of social proceedings in the municipality in which they live. It must be easy, and it must be simple to keep in mind how long it takes to deal with a case. Article 3 (3) (2) The Decree on the Law on legal certainty and administration in the social field provides that the municipal council must set time limits for the period between the receipt of an application and the decision. The time limits shall be published. If the time limit cannot be met in a specific case, the applicant shall be informed in writing of the date on which the applicant can expect a decision. However, today there is no requirement for the deadlines to be published on the municipality's website. Some municipalities use the website to publish the deadlines, but not all of them do, as the municipalities can also choose other ways of informing citizens about the time limits for the proceedings. In order to make it easier for citizens to follow the case in the municipality, we from the Danish People's Party want the municipalities to be obliged to provide information about the processing times on the municipality's website. This uniformity will make it easy and fast for citizens to keep up with, and this will mean greater legal certainty for citizens. The important thing in this context is to be able as a citizen to inform themselves about the level of service in the area of the municipalities and how long it can be expected to take before a decision is taken in the social field. After all, the proposal is not expected to entail additional costs, as it is required to publish it, but not in a particular form or via a particular medium. It may also be claimed that the municipalities' obligation to publish processing times on their website on the Internet is an interference with municipal self-government. That's what we heard today. The proposers believe that this is an extremely limited measure, which should be seen in the light of the considerable improvement in the legal certainty of citizens. In addition, in other contexts the citizen is asked to communicate with the authorities via various self-service options on the Internet, such as Payout Denmark and SKAT. Why, then, is it not also reasonable to ask the municipal authorities to provide relevant information to citizens on their website so that citizens can easily find the information? It is not only common sense, but it is good administrative practice to ensure openness and transparency towards citizens. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119190715	Thank you to the rapporteur. As no one else has asked to speak, the debate is closed. I propose that the proposal be referred to the Committee on Social Affairs and the Internal Market. Are there any comments? It's agreed.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119190732	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for Social Affairs and the Interior.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119190752	Thank you. In this motion for a resolution, B 35, it is proposed that the government should take the initiative to change the possibility of obtaining financial compensation in appeals. In short, the proposal provides for compensation to be paid to citizens in cases where the Ankestyrelsen has agreed with the citizen that the municipality has made a wrong decision under the Service Act. It appears that this is the situation in which a municipality has decided to reduce the assistance under the Services Act received to date by the citizen. According to the proposal, the amount of compensation must be proportionate to the benefit not received by the citizen while the complaint was being examined. According to the motion for a resolution, the system must serve two purposes. Firstly, the compensation must be a plaster on the wound to the citizen for the benefit that the citizen has not had access to during the appeal period. Secondly, the payment of compensation must create an economic incentive for municipalities to make the right decision for the first time. The government agrees that all citizens should receive the help they are entitled to under the law. And they need to get the right decision on the aid in the first place. Having said that, I would also like to say that, in my view, a compensation scheme will be an administratively expensive and cumbersome system which, at the same time, will not help to achieve the result that I believe we agree that we want. The motion for a resolution means that the Ankestyrelsen will then have to use resources to decide on the compensation to be paid to the citizen. And on what basis should such compensation be calculated at all? The kind of benefits that will be affected by the proposal are not money benefits. It is, on the other hand, natural aid, for example. accompanying or helping with practical tasks. And this is a considerable difficulty. As I see it, it will, if necessary, be necessary to have a set of rules for the determination of compensation in different situations, and it just seems very administrative. In the case of monetary benefits under the Services Act, the citizen is already receiving retroactive assistance at the time of application if it subsequently turns out that the municipality's decision was wrong. This is true, for example. decisions on additional costs and lost earnings. In addition, financial compensation in cases of natural aid will not give the citizen what the citizen has wanted and has the right to do, namely help under the Services Act when the need is present. Behind the motion for a resolution there seems to be a premise that the municipalities make decisions deliberately and indiscreetly contrary to the law in order to save aid during a period of complaint and that there is a need to remove an economic incentive to such behaviour. It is an premise that I am a little cautious about simply buying such uncritically, because, as I have also said on many previous occasions, I believe that the municipalities basically have the will to shoulder their responsibility for the fact that the procedure is actually in line with the law. In fact, it is also not the case that the appeal statistics show much less redeployed decisions on, for example. in cases where the benefit is granted retroactively to the citizen by the Ankestyrelsen if the benefit is granted to the citizen than the proportion of converted decisions of the Services Act in general. This should actually be the case if we were to follow the opinion of the proposers that the economic incentive would change something. So I do not think that such a compensation scheme will have the effect of making several correct decisions, and that is why it is not the right way for me to go either. As I have said on several occasions and also on several occasions, the legal certainty of citizens in decisions under the Services Act is, in my opinion, essentially two main elements. The first is that citizens must be able to count on the municipalities to do their best to make correct decisions in the first place. The second is, of course, that citizens should have proper redress if they believe that the municipality has made a decision that is not fully in line with the law. And this is actually where I think we should use our powers. This is where we need to concentrate our efforts. It is the responsibility of the municipalities to ensure that the proceedings are in accordance with the law. On the other hand, it is also important that the municipalities have the best opportunities to fulfil their responsibilities. A number of initiatives have been taken in recent years to support municipal procedures and disseminate knowledge of correct practice. In my view, as I have said, this is a key element in the discussion on how best to ensure legal certainty. I have mentioned the initiatives many times before, and I should just like to mention and highlight here: the current training of municipal case managers and managers by the Ankestyrelsen, the modernisation of the legal hotline of the Agency and the task forces established in the area of children and from 2015 also in the field of disability with experts from the Danish Social Protection Agency and the Danish Agency, who can target the assistance to the municipalities precisely with the aim of obtaining qualified case management in the municipalities. The Ministry also has a review of the guidance on the objectives and general provisions of the Service Act on the way, and this will include: the conditions for reducing a service in connection with changing a municipal service level are clarified. The second element of ensuring the legal certainty of citizens is, as I see it, the right of appeal to the Ankestyrelsen, and here it is crucial that complaints are dealt with within a reasonable period of time so that the citizen can finally clarify what he or she is entitled to. At the same time, it quickly becomes clear to the municipality how the legislation is to be administered in the area in question. Cases have taken too long to be settled in the Ankestyrelsen in the past, but as I have explained several times before, a number of initiatives have been taken to get complaints about municipal decisions dealt with more quickly in the Ankestyrelsen, and this works. In the result plan for the Ankestyrelsen, the time for municipal complaints for 2016 has been set at up to 31⁄2 months, and this is quite different from the 10 months mentioned in the observations made in the motion for a resolution. So it is also important that we in the Folketing take responsibility for the fact that the legislation is understandable to both citizens and municipalities, and also for the fact that it is designed in a way that allows it to be properly administered. That is why, just before the turn of the year, the government announced that we are now launching this work on reforming the adult provisions of the Services Act. And this is, of course, a reform which must be seen in the light of the fact that, in my opinion, our current service law, especially in the adult sector, has become too complex and too difficult for municipalities to administer properly. That is why this reform must result in simplification, but simplification, which must benefit both municipalities and citizens. It must support the fact that the municipalities are looking at the situation of the citizens in one place and are able to give this overall approach, which overall responds to the needs of the citizens. And, of course, there must be a focus on legal certainty. Here I would just mention again that I am putting forward a broad cooperation, among other things. user organisations are involved. They have already been now and here at this early stage. The rapporteurs also know that at the beginning of January I had a good meeting with Danske Handicaporganisations and KL to get the reform work started. I would very much like and believe that it is crucial that we have a good dialogue and that we are aware that we are in the working clothes, because it is about us having to be constructive here, and we also need to have a sensible discussion about the improvements that I think we all agree on the need. So I think that is the right way to go: to create clarity in legislation and to continue to support municipalities in making correct decisions on the basis of professional and economic considerations and on the basis of the needs of the individual and then to work to ensure the prompt handling of complaints. So with this long statement, I would just like to conclude that the government does not support the resolution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119193014	Thank you. The Ankestyrelsen, which succeeded in bringing down the processing times, will be burdened with some rather heavy questions and some completely new questions that the Ankestyrelsen is not geared to take a position on. It is the case that the boards we have typically do not decide on financial compensation, because we have the courts to do so. I also think it is rather unclear what financial compensation should be if necessary. Are we talking about some sort of compensation, or are we talking about the municipality paying it to the citizen that the municipality has saved? It would be quite new to Danish law that we should then go in and introduce what is called punitive damages in American law, that is, that a citizen should be granted financial compensation without the citizen having suffered an economic loss. So the processing time in the Ankestyrelsen will be affected quite negatively by this proposal, and the municipalities will undoubtedly also demand DUT compensation. So we do not agree with the basic distrust of the municipalities, as there is in the motion for a resolution, namely that the municipalities should deliberately choose to take decisions contrary to the law in order to save money. We agree that wrong decisions are being made, and it is also my experience as a lawyer for quite a lot of municipalities that when wrong decisions were made in the municipalities, it was not due to bad will, it was due to complex legislation that was difficult to navigate. It was much more interesting if we got something done about that problem. Then there is something else that is somewhat unclear to me. The Danish People's Party is tabling two motions for resolutions here today: one is suspensory effect and the other is financial compensation. Do you have to have both? Is it necessary to ensure both the suspensive effect, i.e. that a decision is not implemented until the proceedings in the Ankestyrelsen have been completed, and also a financial compensation? And, if so, what is the financial compensation? The citizen has received the help he was entitled to, and no harm has been done, other than that the citizen has of course had to write a complaint, but otherwise the citizen has not suffered any financial loss. It is not quite clear what the proposers really mean here. So it is not the case that we cannot see the problem; we just think that the solution that the Danish People's Party points to is the wrong one. What the government has instead chosen to do is to start work on the reform of the Services Act in the whole adult sector, which, I hope, will result in simplification and debureaucraticisation in the whole area, so that we can ensure that more correct decisions are taken at first instance. This will benefit the municipalities, but it will primarily benefit the citizens. Against this background, therefore, the Left cannot vote in favour of the motion for a resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119201150	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it is the rapporteur of the authors of the motion, Mrs Karina Adsbøl, the Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119201201	Thank you. Thank you for the debate and thank you to the Enhedslisten and the Liberal Alliance, which also supports this and supports the proposal. Thank you, too, for the positive statements that the Danish Parliament is well aware that there are challenges in this area. In the Danish People's Party, we believe that it is reasonable for citizens who are successful in a complaint against the municipality to be granted compensation. Every day the municipalities make decisions under the Social Services Act on matters relating to the elderly and vulnerable citizens, and this can be done, for example. in cases of home help, personal care and the like, and in a number of cases, the decision means that the citizen loses all or part of a good that the citizen has had so far. It can, for example, be done. be that the citizen has been given home help for 6 hours a week, but that the municipality now decides to reduce the home help to e.g. half of it. The municipality must decide on the basis of a concrete and individual assessment of the citizen's situation on the basis of the legislation in force. Unfortunately, we have seen a number of examples of this not always happening. I myself remember a case from a municipality where a ceiling had been set on the day of relief for parents with a disabled child. You cannot set such a ceiling as a municipality, because then you do not make the individual assessment. The reality is that the municipality's economy can be a factor affecting the decision when the municipality has to have the economy to hang together. In many municipalities decisions are made that are at odds with the law, and the municipality may also have an interest in testing the legal framework, and we have seen this in several areas, not only in the field of disability, but also in children's cases. We have also seen it in connection with sickness benefits. If the citizen is not in agreement with or satisfied with the municipal decision, the citizen may appeal against the decision to the municipality, where the matter is reviewed, and if the municipality maintains its decision, the municipality will forward the complaint and the file to the Ankestyrelsen, which will then deal with the matter. In the Danish People's Party, we believe that it is reasonable for the citizen to be granted compensation in cases where the Ankestyrelsen agrees with the citizen's complaint, since during that period the citizen has had to suffer from the municipality's wrong decision and has had to do without the help of the municipality. If, for example, the citizen's complaint concerns reduced home help by 4 hours a week, the citizen has endured this deterioration while the complaint has been pending and if the whole process of complaint has lasted for 10 months, then that help has been lacking, which we believe should trigger a cash compensation. The authority that has made the wrong decision must bear the compensation and the compensation must serve two purposes: firstly, it must be a plaster on the wound to the citizen in relation to the good that the citizen has not had access to and lacked during the appeal period; secondly, it must be a signal to the municipality to be more careful in making the right decision for the first time. A compensation scheme we believe will help create an economic incentive so that the municipality makes the right decision in the first place. The compensation must not, of course, replace any higher compensation which would otherwise be awarded to the complainant. The amount of compensation must also be proportionate to the public's lack of good and the time the citizen has lacked the good and must be measured by the Ankestyrelsen when final decision is taken on the matter. Precisely in relation to this, the Lawyers' Council in the 2015 report, which I previously read out about legal certainty in the administration, has written: "I quote: "Even though the citizen is successful in his complaint after 10-12 months of proceedings in the appeal body, the citizen typically only obtains financial compensation in the form of compensation if the citizen can prove a financial loss. For example, social clients can hardly ever do so simply because they cannot afford to pay for similar private help. ▪ We in the Danish People's Party think this is unfair. And we are convinced that such a compensation scheme in these complaints will increase the incentive for municipalities to make a faster and effective decision, including the right decision, and that it will benefit citizens' legal certainty. A number of things have now been discussed in today's debate, and it is of course no secret that this area, as the disability rapporteur, has been interested in me since 2011, when I was elected to the Folketing, and that is also why I continue to make these motions for resolutions, because it is about something as important as the fact that the citizens can count on us politicians and that we are fighting their case when we see there are absurdities. Now it has been said that the processing times of the Ankestyrelsen are decreasing, but it has also just been said that it was in Avisen.dk that they will be increased if no measures are taken in relation to the relocation of state jobs, and that would certainly be inappropriate. Because I fully agree that the processing times must be reduced, and that was also something we worked on under the previous government, where we were just given additional funds to have the deadlines set by the Ankestyrelsen. However, we must always bear in mind that every time an average is said, we must remember that the citizen may have had the complaint going on for several years. It is so that even if the matter is referred back and forth to the municipality, it is going back and forth, so the average figure is not that one can say that the case has only lasted four months, because often they last much, much longer. I would like to thank you for this debate today and for the positive statements. If nothing else, I hope that we can meet over a joint report in committee.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119202327	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Social Affairs and the Interior. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119202350	Motion for a resolution B 36 proposes that the government should put forward a legislative proposal which gives suspensory effect in appeals to citizens with disabilities who have been deprived of or reduced in aid without the person concerned having improved his or her ability to function. A suspensive effect shall serve two purposes after the motion for a resolution. First of all, the citizen must be given the assistance which the municipality has refused until a decision has been taken by the Ankestyrelsen. Secondly, the scheme must create an economic incentive for municipalities to make the right decision for the first time. The motion for a resolution and the purpose behind it have a number of common features with the motion for a resolution on the compensation system which we have just considered. The general views of the government therefore largely correspond to the views I expressed in relation to the previous motion for a resolution, B-35. We agree that all citizens must have the help they are entitled to under the law and that they must have the right decision on aid in the first place. However, suspensive effect will mean that aid will be provided during the period of appeal, also in the appeals cases where the Ankestyrelsen arrives that the municipality's decision is correct, and that is all else the same in about two thirds of the complaints under the Service Act. With suspensive effect as proposed, a number of citizens will thus have access to benefits to which they do not appear to be entitled, and that seems to me to be less reasonable. This will probably also result in considerable additional public expenditure, and at the same time it may increase the number of complaints, and we in the government therefore do not think it would be appropriate for a complaint to be made to the Ankestyrelsen's suspensive effect as a general rule if the complaint concerns citizens with disabilities who have been deprived of or reduced assistance, as proposed. Indeed, in certain cases where the decision may have a particularly serious impact on the citizen, there is already the possibility of granting suspensory effect to the complaint. In accordance with Article 72 of the Law on legal certainty, the Board of Appeal may, on application, attach a complaint against decisions on settlement offers to adult suspensive effect, and complaints about decisions on placement of children and young people have as a starting point suspensory effect. In addition, a municipality itself or the Ankestyrelsen may, by way of exception, grant a specific appeal suspensory effect if it has far-reaching consequences which are not or are only difficult to recover. The Board of Appeal may also deal with a complaint as a matter of urgency if the Board considers that it is necessary. If a case concerns additional costs or other monetary benefits under the Services Act, the citizen receives the benefit retroactively, if the Ankestyrelsen agrees with the citizen. Finally, it should be borne in mind that the starting point is not that the municipalities suddenly stop all aid to a citizen who has received considerable help so far. Often it will be the case that aid is reduced or changed to another type of aid, so that the citizen continues to receive help, even during the period of complaint. Nor do I believe that a suspensive effect will lead to more real decisions by the municipalities. As I said earlier in connection with B 35 on a compensation scheme, I was somewhat fundamentally opposed to the premise which also seems to be behind B 36, namely that the municipalities make decisions in breach of the law in a fundamental and very conscious manner, in order to save aid during a period of complaint and that there is a need to remove an economic incentive to such behaviour. I still believe that the municipalities have basically the will to shoulder their responsibilities for compliance with the law, and I therefore do not believe that a rule of suspensive effect will affect the decisions of the municipalities in a factual manner. I would repeat that it is important that we in the Folketing take responsibility for the fact that the legislation is understandable to both citizens and municipalities and that it is designed in a way that allows it to be properly administered. That is why, as I have said, the government has decided to start this important work, and I am very, very pleased with the statements that actually come from a great many of the parties in the Danish Parliament, that we are prepared to be here in working clothes. We have started work on a reform of the Services Act's adult regulations, and I have put forward a broad cooperation, among other things. I am also prepared to discuss all good proposals, including proposals for suspensive effects in individual areas, at an early stage. However, it is important to stress that there is also an economic framework to be respected. So I hope that in this way we will jointly be able to find a comprehensive solution for the benefit of citizens who need help under the Services Act. I believe that this is the only right, the only sensible and the only viable way of achieving clarity in the legislation, that we continue to support the municipalities in being able to make correct decisions on the basis of professional and economic considerations and on the basis of the needs of the individual, and that we are also continuing and forward-looking to ensure that complaints are dealt with quickly. So, against this background, the government will not support motion for a resolution B 36.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119204352	Thank you very much, President. I will continue in the highly-voted style by quoting the Constitution, which we have all signed when we were elected to parliament. That's section 63. 1: The courts shall be entitled to rule on any question concerning the limits of the authority. However, anyone who wishes to raise such a question cannot, by bringing the matter before the courts, evade the order of the authorities. ▪ What does that mean in plain English? This means that if an authority, e.g. a municipality, make a decision, then you have the opportunity to go to the courts to check whether that decision was correct. But the fact that you do so does not mean that there will be suspensive effect. So you have to follow the decision, regardless of whether you bring it before the courts. In various areas, there are now some boards, but that does not make the matter any different. Set-up effect is in Danish law the absolute exception. Only in the case of something that cannot be restored shall there be suspensive effect. For example, expropriation: you expropriate a house because you have to build a motorway. The house will be demolished, the highway will be built, and the citizen will agree that the expropriation was illegal. It won't do much good when the house is gone and the highway is built. Therefore, suspensive effects are attributed to such cases. But otherwise it is an absolute exception to impose suspensive effect, and why so? Well, the constitutional fathers have thought about this, for if you could train a decision by bringing it before the Ankestyrelsen or the courts, you would of course do so. We must be very careful in this matter, because we are in the process of fundamentally changing our entire system, and this will lead to an explosion in the number of complaints in this area. And if we do that in this area, what I hope we do not do, then of course it will spread to all sorts of other areas. Then all sorts of others will ask why it should only apply there. Why should this not also apply to other areas where a municipality makes a decision which may subsequently turn out to be illegal? Why should it not also have suspensive effect? The same can be said about the case of financial compensation from before. It will also be a new break, which will result in an explosion in the number of complaints. The easy thing in politics is to point to problems, and the Danish People's Party does, and you point to a real problem. The difficult thing in politics is to make solutions, and this is not a solution. At any rate, this is a solution which will have so great side effects that it will far exceed the positive side of the proposal. Let me say that it is, of course, a pity when a municipality makes a wrong decision, because there is a citizen on the other end that it is going to suffer. And I wonder whether citizens will in future complain, no matter how justified a decision may be. What if you bring the decision of the Ankestyrelsen to the courts? Why, then, should there not be suspensive effect there? I have great sympathy for the fact that the Danish People's Party would like to solve the problems in this area. So the solution you're pointing to is just wrong. There is suspensive effect in cases where it will have a very, very intrusive meaning and where it would be devastating if no suspensive effect were attributed. However, it is not just generally possible to give suspensive effect in all cases. The Danish People's Party will then say: well, we have said that it is only where there is talk of citizens with disabilities having not improved their ability to function. And that is what you have to fight about, i.e. whether or not you have improved your ability to function. So the proposal will result in an explosion in the number of cases in the Ankestyrelsen and longer processing times and an explosion in public expenditure. It is not because we do not sympathize with the problem raised by the Danish People's Party, but because we believe that the solution pointed out is completely wrong.

ParlaMint-DK_20160119205655	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next to speak is Mrs Laura Lindahl, Liberal Alliance. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119205704	Thank you. Motion for a resolution B 36 aims to ensure that suspensive effects are given in appeal proceedings for citizens with disabilities who have been deprived of or reduced in aid, even if they have not had an improved capacity to function. As you can see, this will solve more problems. The citizens will not suddenly be left without help while the complaint is being dealt with and the municipalities will be given an incentive to deal with complaints more quickly, as savings will not be possible during the course of the proceedings. The incentive to deny or reduce aid in violation of the law disappears as the municipalities will not be able to achieve savings in the future while a complaint is pending. Furthermore, legal certainty for people with disabilities will be increased, so that the emphasis will be on social legal certainty rather than on the possibility of the municipal system's budgetary management. If the motion for a resolution is adopted here, citizens with reduced functional capacity will no longer have to suffer the fact that their lives are unduly falling apart while waiting for a complaint to be finalised by the Ankestyrelsen. So this is about being on the side of the citizens with the system, and we are. That is why the Liberal Alliance supports the proposal.

It is gradually both an old and worn travert, which does not necessarily make it truth. Compared with the cashier's check-up, the Ministry of Finance adjusted the financial room for manoeuvre as early as September in connection with the updated cashier's check-up, and yet the argument has been used diligently afterwards. So the so-called black hole with which the government has toured has no hold in reality, and the fact that Denmark continues to spend more than 1%. of GDP on research is overshadowed by the fact that research is cut by 1.4 billion. kr. Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 16 February 2011 laying down the rules and general principles concerning mechanisms for control by Member States of the Commission's exercise of implementing powers (OJ L 55, 28.2.2011, p. 13). The Socialists are therefore constructive in their approach to actions that strengthen research and innovation. At the same time, it is the Social Democrats' ambition to bring research and business closer together. It is vital that we transform knowledge into value. Research results must be brought into play more in the form of new business and export opportunities for Denmark. The Social Democrats are therefore in favour of developing cooperation between knowledge institutions and industry. The barriers to cooperation must be broken down, both cultural and formal barriers, whether they exist. It is the community that finances the new ideas and innovative discoveries of educational establishments; then ideas must also be put into the hands of the community. That is why the Socialists also want to give priority to basic research and to free research. We need to think long-term if we want world-class research that creates value, growth and jobs in Denmark. Basic research and free research are at the heart of this. Unfortunately, in the past six months it has been pointing in a different direction in part. The government's proposals for negotiations on the research reserve were, for example, marked by a major imbalance which would have pulled the curtain away under the Free Research Council. In constructive negotiations, the parties to the agreement succeeded in ensuring the balance in the allocation of funds. This balance must and must also be kept in mind in the future. The statement shows a government with its eyes fixed on today. What should we do to get knowledge and research into gambling in our companies now and today? It is a relevant question, but we must also ask ourselves: what knowledge will be of value tomorrow? That is why there must also be a clear call from the Socialists to the government to give priority to free research. Regionalisation must be carried out wisely. We must ensure a Denmark in balance, but we must not undermine already existing knowledge and innovation environments. At the same time, the Social Democrats are highly critical of the fact that the Minister has removed the focus on social mobility in higher education development contracts. Social mobility in the education sector is a crucial element in the overall social mobility of society, and the Social Democrats are opposed to the government having abandoned that objective. That's what we saw with the project for the research reserve. At the same time, too one-sided focus on commercialisation means that you risk losing focus on research that is at least as important on other parameters. Research into welfare, social heritage and other social contexts may not have an immediate commercial purpose, but the knowledge it can provide can be of equal value. If profit becomes the sole ruler of research, we run the risk of missing out on gains that we cannot imagine today. We should be careful about taking that route. That is why the Socialists call on the government to maintain the balance between commercial research on the one hand and the research foundation on the other. In relation to the proposal on rural pilots, there are good tactics in the proposal, which could, however, be usefully made nationwide. The Left rapporteur said, I think, very wisely that we in Denmark have stepped on the accelerator and that it has worked. Unfortunately, in the meantime, the foot has been lifted and instead placed on the brake. It is the Socialists' hope that the Left Government will return to giving real priority to the area, so that future discussions in the field of research and innovation policy will not be about billion cuts, but about how we will strengthen the whole sector from the point of view, no one knew the need, to the day-to-day application of our businesses, which are creating jobs and growth here and now. Our research-based programmes across the country must have the best students, employees and researchers who are both able to acquire the latest knowledge and translate it for the benefit of the whole society. It is actually a very important point and something very special about Denmark that we have a great common public research because it is independent and can serve the interests of society wider and better. Another feature of Denmark is that we have had a tradition of prioritising free research and basic research. It is a research which is neither strategically determined by politicians nor by business, but which is only framed by the researchers' own creativity and ingenuity. So it has placed us in world class in the field of research. So when the Minister of Research in this paper points out that more research needs to be done for business, it is a problem because at the same time it means that there will be a corresponding reduction in free research and basic research. And it is in free research and basic research that unpredictable and experimental research, which can deliver the great breakthroughs and results of the future, is taking place. So by focusing only on the very immediate interests that businesses can have here and now, we are in danger of missing out on some knowledge and inventions in which we, as a society, have a very great interest in the longer term. And what I hear from researchers, whom I know, is that there is now little time left to do research when, at the same time, we also have to deal with large amounts of bureaucracy and administration and write applications and teach. In the statement here, the Minister talks about the need for research to be more effective and accessible. I would just like to call for attention to be paid at the same time to how new requirements will affect the quality of research. There are studies that show that researchers' everyday lives in e.g. The biotech industry in both Denmark and the United States has become much harder. They do not have proper time to research because of increasing demands for cooperation with industry and contact with the public and the fact that they must constantly apply for new money with all the administration it entails. So that leaves very little time for real research. So I have to say that all in all we have a picture that what the government has done in the field of research and education since it came into being is so short-term and so unambitious and harmful that one can be a little depressed about it. Our task is to ensure that there is a good financial framework for research and we must have confidence that researchers can actually best deliver without too much political interference. ParlaMint-DK_20160119214853	I'm Henrik Dahl of the Liberal Alliance for the next 10 minutes because he was prevented from being here. In the American university world, a number of years ago, there was a discussion of critical research into administrative research. First a little about the critical. Being the safe base of free and critical thought is the very nature of the universities. It is this protection of freedom of thought which has historically made European universities special and which has led all prosperous parts of the world to use the European model as a basis for developing their own. Critical research is necessarily and always revolutionary. Can it now also be true what the greatest and the most reputable in one's own profession say? With this question critical researchers have at all times begun their work. Critical research often leads to bitter disagreements between the old and the new thinking, but it is such bitter disagreements that in the long run drive knowledge further. So little about administrative research. Being an extended instrument for product development was what the conciliation circle behind the current university law wanted the universities to be. This is also evident from the Minister's statement. But is it critical research to make a slightly better version of what already exists, or is it not rather what is called administrative research in the American university world, that is. research, where you know exactly where you want to end up, but just don't know the way yet. That is why one of the questions we are asking today is: how does the Minister feel that the critical and rebellious research that is destroying existing world images is doing so? It is true that Denmark still adheres to the Barcelona objective of setting aside 1%. of GDP for research, but of course a 7-percent saving sends shock waves through the university world because so much of the budgets are tied in fixed costs. However, it has been shown that research savings indirectly hit other activities very hard, and why do they do so? They do, because universities have been charging a kind of 44 per cent tax for years. of all the research appropriations received from the State. Without this so-called tax, which the universities call overhead, the other activities cannot be linked at all, and therefore it hits the universities harder when the tax base shrinks. On the political side, or at least on the part of the Liberal Alliance, the problem with this whole system is that there is a lack of transparency. If you don't know about this so-called tax, you're tempted to believe that when the state grants 1 million. kr., gets the researcher 1 million. kr., but he or she doesn't. In fact, only DKK 560,000 is owed to the researcher. out of 1 million kr. The rest, which is far more than the administration of the specific research project can cost, is sent around the system and used for anything other than research. So the question is: does the Minister think this so-called tax system is fair and fair, and what is the Minister really going to do to promote transparency in these matters? Henrik Dahl would say that it is a good rule of thumb that if a composite noun consists of more than four elements, there is something wrong. And here we are up to five. But to be serious, this is all about the state not creating economic growth. Private companies do. Or less relevant things are measured, such as: the number of meetings and seminars held or people's subjective experiences of the activities? Some elements stand to a high grade and others to a lower. The minister writes in the statement that the government's wish is that Danish researchers and research environments should be in the absolute top class, especially in areas that match business needs. And here, in the alternative, we could not disagree more with the government. For yes, research must also benefit the business community, but not only, and certainly not, as a primary objective. For what is really the DNA of research and the primary objective of research? Is it to ensure economic growth and jobs? No, we don't think so. It is to generate knowledge that can provide free and critical and innovative and provocative and remarkable and amazing perspectives on some of the structures that exist and legislation and standards, etc. That is the essence of research. Such knowledge will just never be generated if researchers, for example. must spend a disproportionate amount of time writing applications for new projects, while completing the research projects they are in the process of, because they must secure new funds before the funding for the old funds runs out. This creates a lack of time for real research, and in fact it also creates a competitive working environment with secret drafting of applications on the other side of the wall, because the colleague next door actually sits and writes on applications for the same funds as I do, for example. Doing in my office. It is unsightly, and it is actually exhausting in relation to a good working environment. And I know because I've been in it myself. So if we now ask ourselves the question: what are the consequences for research when it is directed primarily at business? What kind of research do we get if the success parameter is growth and jobs? We shall then have an answer which I think is in direct contradiction with the nature of research, namely that research is no longer free but governed by a specific purpose and therefore in a particular direction. We can look at some of the best researchers that Denmark has actually produced. One is Jens Christian Skou, who received the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 1997. He writes in his memoirs of 2003 that the freedom of research in today's research policy is undermined, and he tells how he had time for reflection in his research, because there were fixed appropriations, and because one was allowed to test his theses and pursue his wonders without having to account for research that did not produce an unambiguous or immediately usable result. And these are exactly the conditions, he says, which were actually the prerequisite for him to get the Nobel Prize in chemistry. Another example is Eske Willerslev, as we all know, the DNA researcher, who in 2014 was admitted to the National Academy of Sciences, this fine, distinguished American company for researchers. He is behind the discovery that it was the North Atlantic Indians who were the first people in America. Is it research that benefits Danish business? I don't think so. Is it research that creates jobs? I don't think so. But is it good and qualified research that belongs at the top of the research league? It certainly is. So the understanding of research, as I see it from this statement, has many similarities with what we all know as the new public management idea. Academies have already been rolled into this mindset years ago, where researchers have to apply for virtually everything, where everything has to be counted, and where what counts is virtually exclusively how many scientific articles you have in international journals and reach as well, just how many times you are quoted by the other researchers. The government's plans, which are referred to in the report, to launch an analysis programme with the intention of creating knowledge about the effects of public research funds, with the aim of prioritising them so as to support the needs of business, seem to me to be a screaming example of such a new public management approach, and I think it is really a pity for research. So which way is research going to follow? Now I have spent a great deal of time trying to correct the Minister's statement, and it is of course also appropriate that I explain what we in the Alternative would like to see as the future path for Danish research. First of all, like others, we want to eliminate all the excessive bureaucracy in the research institutions. There must be no more documenting in the head and in the back, and respect for the slow work must go back. We must give researchers time to go into depth, to wonder and to pursue the path of inspiration rather than to choose strategically according to where the money is most. This requires that we also set the research institutions much more free and that we trust that the institutions manage the funds with a high level of professionalism and with respect for the commitment and zeal of the individual researcher. In other words, we want to set research free and we believe that research is useful because what inventions lead to free research? Yeah, that's the good question, because we don't actually know that. That is why we need to have the courage to believe in it and to be willing to bet. There are no guarantees, but there is a huge potential for free research. This is, in fact, the way many groundbreaking discoveries have come to light, among other things. The penicillin is an example of this. So we must dare to rely on free research. Does this mean that, in the alternative, we want to set research completely free? In fact, it does not, but we want to free a greater part of the research. But we must be the first to admit that we also have goals for a better world, and a better world in our optics is not just about strengthening the economic bottom line of business. Our goals for a better world are primarily to create a sustainable future where green energy replaces black energy and where pressures on the planet's resources are reduced. Future Danish research must provide answers to the climate crisis, simply, and so massive research and investment in green conversion and in circular economy is needed. This, together with a massive upgrading of free research, is an ambitious plan for the future of Danish research. I fully agree that it is certainly not the Minister's ambition to follow that plan, so I will end up with a few proposals instead of just making a few more points in the Minister's statement. Firstly, there is a clash in the statement between the government's plans to ensure that research is to be carried out throughout Denmark, in other words with a particular focus on rural areas, and then the cuts we have seen in education, which are to a large extent the result of the dismantling of some rural educational institutions. So I would suggest to the Minister that he sit down with the Minister of Education and find out how these considerations can be reconciled. Secondly, we are actually confident in the alternative that Danish companies are not only made up of people who think in profit, but who also have a purpose and a will and some vision for the company they have, and who also want to make a difference between the social and environmental bottom line for Denmark. So we are actually proposing that the government should give companies much better opportunities, without risking the economic bottom line, and also to bid on the social and environmental bottom line. And a concrete idea here could actually be to dress these innovation agents, as mentioned in the statement, to do innovation checks that involve not only the economic bottom line, but also the social and environmental bottom line. As you can hear, we're rich in ideas in the Alternative... this was just two of them... and I hope the Minister is listening. And our door is always open if the minister wants to hear more about our visions for the future of Danish research. With that I would like to thank you for the floor.
