In fact, I think it is one of the most important things in the report, because it says that research is the driving force for a society in development. I think that the rapporteur of the alternative said it very well, namely that research is so at the heart of everything we think and in the good ideas. I think that was quite well said. We believe that not all research needs to be directed directly towards business or have a direct purpose in business in order to have an important function in our society. We think that strategic research can have its advantages, but we think that it is also in free research that you find some of the more critical and alternative research projects, where you may have thought, from the outset, that what ever returns is going to come, and then suddenly it was some ideas that ended up changing the world and that suddenly gave a huge return for Denmark. Recovery is a whole section of the report, and as such it is a fun concept, as I also fear we will be discussing a lot in relation to grants to universities and business services. But that is another discussion, and yes, some things can make private money on, but I think that we should both give room to those things that may well give a return one day, but also to the crooked ideas. We have a good education system in the country, and we therefore have many talented research talents, which, in addition to having great courage, have many original ideas that we must pursue and help to realise. We must create spaces to pursue original research pages with an unknown potential to improve our life and way of life. It is actually one of the descriptions you can see if you go to the Free Research Council's website. And I think it's quite apt. Well, there are some things where you think that yes, this can come out, and it can give a return and help strengthen Denmark in international competition. But there are also some research pages that again are the cores of a society in development that can just help improve our way of life in this room and so on throughout society. I think that is important to recognise. Another part of the research, which is also mentioned in this report and which we have also discussed a great deal, is the green demonstration programmes. After all, it is a bit forward and backward that it can be used for commercialisation, and there is again this recovery, but I think it is actually a success, because on the one hand it creates jobs and provides a return, but most importantly it helps to contribute to the green transition. That's why we fought hard, it's no secret to the research reserve to get more funding for the green demonstration programmes. For, unfortunately, some of them were also said to be the ones to which we are reducing the allocation. Water technology, which is part of the demonstration programmes, which we find very exciting, is an important area to target research, we believe that together we can ensure clean drinking water, wastewater treatment and reduce emissions of climate gases in the water sector. The environment, both in the vicinity and in the distance, is subject to many negative influences, which requires us as a local community, but also as a parliament and world society, to act actively to change our current way of dealing with nature. So our recommendation will be that we continue to really focus on these development demonstration programmes, because we believe that there is a lot to get. Now I have tried to focus a little on many of the good things and some of what is important for the SF, but I also need to add a little more words to what has happened with the Finance Act. For as much as I am for the transparency that lies in this statement, I am just as sorry about the direction the government has taken in Denmark's research ambitions. From the first year of life, 1.4 billion have been saved. kr. In the field of research. I remember that the good Villy Søvndal called it a welfare sacchar in some welfare areas, but I think it is right to talk about this being a research massacre in some areas. It has been so hard that the mentioned Enhedslisten's rapporteur has also been so hard that Danish companies, some of those who create the most jobs, have been threatening to leave the country because they fear that they will not be able to get the trained labour they need and the knowledge they need. I really think that should be taken into account in future reflections, and I hope that when the government assesses it I think that we have different assessments of when there will be an economy for it soon to assess that there is an economy to give more to research. To cut the research is like peeing in your pants to stay warm. Denmark needs the good ideas, and if it is up to SF, Denmark will become even more a pioneer country when it comes to strong research environments. Then I have a very specific question which I have been thinking about a little. I didn't think it was big enough for it to be worth a consultation, but then came this statement, and then we thought it would be a great opportunity. In fact, as I understand it, we have a rental system in which universities pay rent. Now I read up: The rental system is designed so that a small half billion kroner annually flows back to the Treasury from the total public funding for research and development. This is not money that goes to maintain or manage university buildings, it is a purely return requirement that the state has chosen to collect from the university sector. Parliament formally sells 20.6 billion. kr. for research and development in 2016. It is not a random number, and it has also been mentioned several times today. It's because you see this 1.01 target that we've committed ourselves to in Barcelona. However, as the figures for university rent show, part of the grant also goes towards the return on rent, i.e. they go back into the treasury, as we see it, and if you correct it, the real research and development allocation is only 20.15 billion. kr., because you send some money back to the Treasury and so don't spend it on research. That's 0.988 per cent. It is therefore slightly below the target to which we have committed ourselves. I would just like to say that it is quite clear that we have some money that we are giving to research and that is coming back to the treasury again, and I think that you are actually so excited that you do not reach your Barcelona target. I would like to know what the Minister thinks about it. It is, moreover, the University newspaper that has described it in an article and done a good job. ParlaMint-DK_20160119222244	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then I give the floor to the Minister for Education and Research.
ParlaMint-DK_20160119222256	I would also like to begin by expressing my support for the many good considerations underlying the speeches that have been made here. Although it is a relatively late hour, it is a pleasure, regardless of whether you look at research and innovation policy statements from one political spectrum or another, to hear that there has been a great deal of reflection on where you want to move it here. Only last week I hosted the annual research and innovation meeting. About 100 key players in the field participated in Kolding. The theme at the meeting was from knowledge to value, and we had some very good discussions, speeches and reflections on how we get the good research that we do in Denmark out to benefit society. Making research useful is a subject which concerns me very much and which is also at the heart of the statement that we have been discussing this evening. It is not only the economic part of research as a value-creator, but also, to a large extent, the human part. Of course, in a research and innovation policy statement based on the original agreement on the Danish Innovation Fund, there is a focus on the more economic growth element. I very much agree with the rapporteurs who have spoken about research as a major driving force for the development of our society. Danish research is to bring information, growth and jobs to the whole of Denmark. In this context, it is not only the size of investments that is important, but also our ability to transform research-based knowledge into innovative products and processes, spin-off companies and thus growth and jobs, because research must be of benefit. That's the government's objective. We've gone to the polls to invest at least 1%. of GDP in research and development. The status is that despite the savings made in the autumn, we are still investing a great deal of money in public research, and all the indications are that Denmark will continue to be one of the countries in the OECD that has the largest public research budget as a share of gross domestic product in 2016. At the same time, the business community invests considerable sums, which means that we have a very, very high level of funding in Denmark when it comes to our research. In just a few years, the proportion of our funding for research in Denmark has increased by 50 per cent, which means that despite the fact that we have had a decline in appropriations for 2016, we are significantly higher than in the 1990s or in the early 00s, for example. All of this is an indication that, as a society, we are not disappearing from the research elite, we are really, really high today, even if we have had savings. The question then is whether we will get enough out of the investments we are making. As can be seen from the report, Danish research and innovation have a good starting point, because Danish research is in a strength position, and this is confirmed by the latest figures from the Research Barometer from 2015. We are generally doing well in the international comparison, among other things. is Denmark's R & D in top three, if you look at the number of publications in relation to population • only Switzerland and Iceland produce several scientific publications per year. population than Denmark. At the same time, Danish research is generally of high international quality and in a number of areas, for example. in food, climate and pharmaceutical research, Danish researchers are in the world elite. This is also evident when you look at the proportion of the Danish research publications, which are among the 10%. most quoted articles. When it comes to innovation, we are doing well too. We're number one. 2 after Sweden on the EU Innovation Union Scoreboard from 2015, which compares the innovation capacity of EU countries. I am proud that Danish research has such high quality and impact, but I am also very concerned that we are even better at getting research out to create value in our society. As I said, both human and economic value. Although we are already getting much out of the research that is being carried out today, we need to get even better. Today, for example, Denmark is only in the middle field when it comes to the number of new inventions, patents and licensing, sales and option agreements from public research institutions. Of course, this does not show the overall picture because a very important factor is missing. For the most important knowledge bridge between research and industry, the graduates from higher education are employed in Danish companies every year. It is all their knowledge that will be useful in the Danish companies. But well can be better, because despite the high quality of Danish research, we must also be able to translate that research into new solutions, processes, products and services. Basically, it is about creating a better interaction between public research and the surrounding society, so that we increase the cake so that there is more to be shared out. This year's report focuses on the interaction between business and public research with a particular regional focus. Public research efforts must meet the needs of business for new knowledge. It is not the case that all research should be targeted at the needs of industry now and here, because much research only discovers the usefulness of after many years, as several of the rapporteurs have also pointed out, and the companies of the future will be some other than those we know today. But we need to be better at using our research knowledge in companies. This supports the government's objective of creating growth and more private jobs throughout Denmark. Danish companies must have access to new knowledge and competences, and the entire research and innovation system must be geared to supporting knowledge collaboration between companies and research institutions, wherever they are in the country. The report focuses specifically on the instruments that support this interaction. These may include joint research projects and training of PhDs, as well as smaller initiatives including counselling, sparring and matchmaking. The means of action must constitute a coherent system, it must be transparent for companies and other users, what instruments are relevant to them, and regional and national initiatives must complement each other. In the government, we have been focusing from the first day on the fact that growth and development are not only beneficial to large cities. We should not divide Denmark into development and dismantling, and since innovation is a major driving force for business development, this also applies, of course, to the research and innovation system. The report shows that business research and development is concentrated on Copenhagen and Aarhus, while business innovation activities are more evenly distributed across Denmark. At the same time, there are significant regional differences between the services offered by the companies. It is mainly companies in the Capital Region and partly East Jutland that participate in the major projects and international programmes. The companies participating in innovation networks and smaller projects are more equally located throughout Denmark. It may be an exaggeration that demand for knowledge and skills varies regionally and that a differentiated research and innovation system is needed to meet the specific needs of businesses in the different regions. We need to be aware of this, and this is a topic on which I, as Minister for Education and Research, focus and will focus further in the new year. Let us then look at some of the things that will take place in 2016. There is a clear link between the skills of employees and the ability of companies to develop and translate knowledge into innovation. Education is perhaps the most important source of innovation in companies, new graduates take their knowledge and ideas from the studies with them into the companies, and we in the government want this to take place throughout the country • also companies outside the major cities should be able to attract well qualified and highly trained labour. Therefore, with the growth plan, the government has set up a rural growth pilot scheme under the auspices of the Danish Innovation Fund. The government wants to provide economic support to small and medium-sized enterprises in rural areas so that they can hire a highly trained growth pilot to solve a concrete innovation project. Businesses located in areas outside the major cities employ less highly trained workers. With the growth pilots, we get highly trained employees out in Denmark, and it is a springboard for more development, growth and job creation in companies. At the same time, with the growth plan, the government has proposed that the Innovation Fund should have employees associated with strong regional knowledge environments, for this enables the Innovation Fund to support research and innovation across the country with a strong regional anchoring through a physical presence. As the report shows, the bridge building between public research institutions and companies succeeds best around the major cities. In the rest of Denmark, we have been less good at connecting the bridge to business, so we need to look at that. Small and medium-sized enterprises across the country must have easier access to knowledge from public research institutions, and therefore the government is proposing a new objective in educational institutions' development contracts that enhance knowledge cooperation. In this way, we will strengthen regional business promotion efforts and the institutions will have the opportunity to test their research in practice. At the same time, as something new, the government will introduce development objectives to the GTS. Among other things, it has to be done. support that we reap the full potential of the knowledge developed at the research institutions. GTS play a major role when it comes to building an infrastructure of knowledge, skills and equipment that is made available to all Danish companies. This is to be seen in the wake of the fact that, at the beginning of 2016, we have concluded new performance contracts with the GTS institutions of just under 1 billion. kr. over a 3-year period. Some of the contracts will focus on increasing Danish influence on international standardisation in areas with significant potential for Danish small and medium-sized enterprises. There will also be a special effort to promote cooperation with foreign institutions so that we can improve the access of Danish companies to technological services.
